Netflix's 8-Part Female John Wick Series Is One Of Its Best Binges

📅 Nov 14, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

It’s rare for any thriller to live up to the John Wick franchise, let alone a small-screen show, but one Netflix series with a single season managed this feat. It is tough to think of an action thriller movie from the last 15 years that was more influential than 2014’s John Wick. In terms of pure plot, the Keanu Reeves vehicle was the simple story of a retired assassin killing every criminal involved in his dog’s death.

However, in visual and aesthetic terms, John Wick spelled the death knell for the shaky cam style that dominated action thrillers in the 2000s and early 2010s thanks to the popularity of director Paul Greengrass’s groundbreaking Bourne sequels. After John Wick’s release, the movie’s sequels popularized a hyper-smooth, slick action filmmaking style that prioritized gliding, unbroken Steadicam shots over fast-cutting.

Soon enough, Atomic Blonde, Bullet Train, Extraction, the Nobody movies, Kate, Gunpowder Milkshake, Pretty Lethal, Violent Night, and almost every other action thriller movie had embraced this CGI-assisted filmmaking style. Few of these efforts could match the John Wick sequels, and even John Wick’s own streaming spinoff The Continental ended up feeling like a knock off rather than a legitimate franchise entry. This is why it is so impressive that Netflix’s under-seen South Korean thriller series My Name recaptured John Wick’s entire appeal perfectly.

As an action thriller about revenge, My Name sounds straightforwardly similar to John Wick in terms of plot. However, the complicated lore of the show’s story makes My Name closer in story terms to the Internal Affairs movies or their Americanized re-imagining, The Departed. Han So-hee’s Yoon Ji-woo is the daughter of a powerful mob member who joins the city's police so she can act as a mole and pass on information to the Dongcheon drug cartel.

Yoon Ji-woo’s boss, the kingpin Choi Mu-jin, encourages her to spy on the police, meaning the show’s heroine soon lands a spot on the very task force that is investigating the cartel. However, once there, she learns from her new detective partner that Choi Mu-jin is suspected to have killed her father. With split loyalties, Yoon Ji-woo has no one to trust and every reason to sharpen her combat skills. While My Name’s plot is as complex as any crime drama like Love/Hate, this show is no slow burn, realistic character study.

Instead, Netflix’s hyper-stylized My Name is a neon-drenched thrill ride, as Yoon Ji-woo battles her way through the city’s criminal underworld while trying to solve the mystery of her father’s sudden death. Gritty, dark, and fast-paced, the action show is a must-watch for fans of revenge thrillers.

Every action show needs to find the right balance between constant fights, chases, and gun battles, but My Name’s tense premise gives the story an incredible head start in this regard. My Name’s twisty plot and the complicated machinations of its heroine’s plan mean that even the show’s quiet dialogue scenes crackle with constant tension.

There is always a risk that Yoon Ji-woo will be exposed as a mole in either organization, which ramps up tension during quiet scenes and makes the action all the more hard hitting. Even compared to dark crime thrillers like This City Is Ours, My Name’s one-season story is a masterclass in tension building.

On that note, the show’s one-season status actually works for, rather than against, My Name as a series on the whole. While it would have been hard to drag My Name’s story out for more than one season, Netflix’s killer John Wick replacement wisely limited its story to just one perfectly paced outing instead.

RHOP’s Karen Huger Shares Update on Ray Huger Marriage After Season 11

📅 Nov 14, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Real Housewives of Potomac's Karen Huger exclusively told E! News at the Hunting Wives season two premiere where she stands with husband Ray Huger as they plan the holidays with daughter Rayvin.

Karen Huger has no illusions of grandeur about the future of her marriage.

While the Real House of Potomac's Grand Dame admitted viewers would have to stay tuned to see how she and husband Ray Huger continue to work on their relationship during season 11, she confessed issues with her partner of 32 years haven't gone away.

"We're going through a rough patch," Karen exclusively told E! News at the Hunting Wives season two premiere Nov. 12, crediting her ability to face their struggles to the fact that "Ray and I have always been honest."

And despite the difficulties in her relationship since her return home after serving six months in prison due to a 2024 DUI conviction, the reality star has a positive outlook about their family unit with daughter Rayvin Huger.

As Karen explained, "The one thing is, we will always be a family, and we will always be friends."

And because of the couple's unbreakable bond, their plans for the holidays remain a family affair where the motto is the more the merrier.

"We're going to celebrate as a family," Karen shared. "In fact, we are going down to Virginia, near the guest house that I just renovated, to celebrate with our entire family."

As for how the 63-year-old feels about her equally complicated relationship with costar Wendy Osefo?

"I'm hoping for the best for Wendy. I'm always in her corner," Karen remarked. "We are supporting her 100 percent and wishing for the best."

As Karen put it, "Brittany Snow? Love, love, love. Sign me up. I'm ready to kill someone, OK."

With that in mind, Karen is open to trying out her acting chops alongside the Hunting Wives cast, musing when it comes to a future cameo, "All things are possible."

Rinnai’s new heat pump water heaters get smarter – and easier to install

📅 Nov 14, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Rinnai has launched a new lineup of electric heat pump water heaters that adds wifi controls and more installation options to an appliance that can cut the energy needed for hot showers.

The REHPW series comes in 50-, 65-, and 80-gallon sizes. It builds on Rinnai’s existing REHP lineup, with changes designed to make the units easier to install, monitor, and maintain.

Heat pump water heaters pull heat from the surrounding air and transfer it into the tank. Moving that heat requires less electricity than producing it with conventional electric resistance elements. There’s no fuel combustion at the appliance, although emissions from its electricity supply depend on the grid.

Rinnai lists a Uniform Energy Factor, or UEF, of 3.75 for the 50-gallon model and 4.0 for the two larger versions. Higher numbers mean greater efficiency under standardized testing; actual bills depend on electricity rates, hot water use, and installation conditions.

The lineup delivers up to 91 gallons of hot water in the first hour, depending on tank size. That rating includes both stored hot water and what the unit can heat during that hour – useful context when several people take consecutive showers.

There are five operating modes, 4.5 kW backup heating elements, and a variable-speed fan designed to keep noise down. The heat pump can operate with ambient air as low as 37°F.

Installation is where some of the more practical upgrades show up.

The new Rinnai heat pump water heater units have water connections on both the top and side, giving installers more options for connecting existing plumbing. Both the air intake and exhaust are on top, allowing zero side clearance where Rinnai’s installation requirements permit.

An optional ducting kit adds flexibility for tighter spaces. Carrying handles help with moving the tank into place, and the air filter slides out horizontally from the side for cleaning.

Rinnai has also added a third tank temperature sensor to the 50- and 80-gallon models to help manage hot water delivery.

Wifi adds remote monitoring and control. Rinnai’s updated Central app supports the REHPW series, including scheduling and tools to use time-of-use electricity rates to manage energy use.

The units also carry CTA-2045 AC Level 2 certification for demand response. That gives compatible utility programs a way to communicate with the water heater and adjust its electricity use. Rinnai says the certification can help qualify homeowners for local and state utility demand-response rebates; availability depends on the program.

The series is Energy Star certified, meets NEEA Tier 4 requirements, and complies with California’s Title 24 requirements, including JA13, according to Rinnai. Warranty coverage includes 10 years for the tank and other parts, plus one year for labor.

Rinnai directs buyers to an installer for pricing, so the upfront cost – and how much a local rebate might offset it – will require a quote. However, the REHP series price on Amazon hovers around the $2,000 mark.

3 Best Movies To Watch On Prime Video This Weekend (November 14-15)

📅 Nov 14, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

From that lineup, only the latter is currently trending on the streaming service nationwide, as the post-apocalyptic thriller Apocalypse Z: New World currently stands as the 9th most popular movie on Prime Video's Top 10 Movies chart in the United States. As such, it remains a good option as we head into the weekend, just like the first film in the franchise, Apocalypse Z: The Beginning of the End.

Last weekend's recommendations were a comedy, a psychological thriller, and a post-apocalyptic thriller. This time around, I have shifted the list towards sci-fi, with one of the films being a masterpiece cyberpunk release that stands as one of the most iconic sci-fi movies to have ever been released. Joining it is an action thriller with a sci-fi twist to it.

The only non-sci-fi movie on our new Prime Video list is a spy comedy, which promises to be the film for the whole family on the streaming service this weekend. These are the best movies to watch on Prime Video this weekend.

Starting the list is the most different release, Agent Cody Banks, the spy comedy starring Frankie Muniz and Hilary Duff, which is like a teenaged James Bond adventure. Given how the casting of Daniel Craig's replacement as James Bond is the talk of the town in Hollywood, with actors like Robert Pattinson and Nicholas Hoult joining the race, I felt that Agent Cody Banks was a timely choice.

That said, do not expect the film to go as dark and gritty as many of the 007 movies, given how Agent Cody Banks relies heavily on Muniz's comedic timing from his experience in the sitcom Malcolm in the Middle. This one is worth watching for the leads' chemistry, the spy gadgets, the fast-paced action, and the light take on material like James Bond.

Infinite starts to lean our list towards sci-fi, with the Mark Wahlberg release currently standing as the most popular movie on Prime Video in the United States. Infinite is the perfect option for those who are looking for action this weekend, but want something grittier and a lot more mature than Agent Cody Banks.

Infinite follows Wahlberg as Evan McCauley, a man who deals with mysterious visions, has abilities that he does not remember training to develop, and more. However, when he learns from a group, the Infinites, that these visions are memories of past lives, everything changes, and he must face a dangerous foe. Infinite is not groundbreaking, but the film is worth watching for its intense action and talented ensemble, which also includes Dylan O'Brien, Chiwetel Ejiofor, Jason Mantzoukas, and more.

Finally, Blade Runner: The Final Cut was one of the early October arrivals on Prime Video, and anyone who follows the masterpiece sci-fi cyberpunk franchise started by director Ridley Scott must know why it is a timely movie. After all, Prime Video's Blade Runner 2099 series will be released on the streaming service on November 25, so now is the time to start the franchise or rewatch it, if you have not already.

This first Blade Runner movie set up an immersive and intricate world where the line between human and machine is blurred. Harrison Ford delivers a magnetic performance in the role of Rick Deckard, and the film helped set the tone for live-action cyberpunk movies and TV shows. All those factors make the morally complex thriller a must-watch on Prime Video this weekend.

Amtrak booking system experiencing major issues amid nationwide FAA ground stop

📅 Nov 14, 2026  |  Category: Travel  |  Read Source →

WASHINGTON - Amtrak's ticket booking system is reportedly down Friday evening, as thousands of travelers across the U.S. deal with a nationwide FAA ground stop.

An error message on Amtrak's website while attempting to book a ticket on Friday, Nov. 13, 2026.

Users on social media started sharing frustrations over ticket reservations Friday afternoon, with travelers showing error messages on Amtrak's website and app.

Hundreds of users have reported issues on DownDetector, beginning shortly before 5 p.m. Friday, with another surge just before 6 p.m.

In response to a request for comment, Amtrak's Communications Director W. Kyle Anderson said that "Amtrak service is operating normally, and we are seeing increased demand for travel across our network."

Responding to a frustrated customer Friday, Amtrak officials said in a post on X that the booking issues were related to a system-wide update, but did not elaborate.

FOX 5 DC attempted to call the support number listed with the error message. An automated message said officials were unable to take calls due to "unusually high call volume."

A nationwide ground stop has been issued by the the FAA due to a "temporary telecommunications outage."

The outage comes as thousands of travelers across the country are scrambling for new plans, after the Federal Aviation Administration issued a nationwide ground stop — the first since January 2023 and just the second since the Sept. 11, 2001, attacks.

The FAA has said that the ground stop was ordered due to a "temporary telecommunications outage."

Painter Sascha Braunig Pushes Through the Void

📅 Nov 14, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Sascha Braunig’s paintings have a tendency to produce visceral reactions: a pit in the stomach, an eyebrow raise, a need to move closer. This is not unintended.

“I want the viewer to have a bodily experience,” Braunig says.

The paintings are bright, smooth, candy-colored—lighthearted contrasts to the psychologically intense shapes: luminously rendered heads and torsos often bound by wiry tentacles. At times the figures recede into their matching optic backgrounds, as if hiding in plain sight.

Braunig, who is 43 and splits her time between New York and Maine, has been painting with this funky verve for close to two decades. Not long after she got her MFA from Yale’s painting program in 2008, she was having solo exhibitions of her small paintings of heads at the now-defunct Foxy Production in Manhattan. Inclusions at the New Museum Triennial and MoMA PS1 soon followed. (Attention from critics came quickly as well; one called her an “inspired reanimator of Surrealism,” another a “phenom.”)

Braunig is excellent at capturing the inherent tension of living in a body—especially one that is subjected to societal expectations of femininity. She’s playing with the ways women have been contorted and cinched and squeezed for time immemorial, asked to fit into archetypes that seem at turns glamorous or sinister. Often they are both.

“Our aesthetic, our personhood is formed in relation to these cultural forces and imagery,” she says. “You can’t separate yourself out from what you’ve seen throughout your whole life.”

These themes of gender performance and corporeal control permeate “Wrist Painter,” Braunig’s most extensive exhibition to date, which opens November 13 at the Portland Art Museum. The show brings together some 45 works, including paintings, sculptures, works on paper, and sketchbooks. It will be her first solo institutional show in Maine. (A special occasion, as almost all of the pieces in the show were made a block and a half away, in her studio at the Portland arts nonprofit SPACE, over the last 15 years.)

“Sascha really stands out here in this arts ecosystem. She’s doing something really singular,” says Sayantan Mukhopadhyay, a curator at PMA who organized the show. When people think of Maine painters, they usually think of landscapes. But Braunig leans toward the cerebral, with a bit more bite. “The paradoxes are what really excite me about the work,” Mukhopadhyay says.

And the dichotomies are many. “Sascha has a way of presenting an abstraction that can read both soft and strong, feminine and violent,” says Olivia Smith, whose New York City gallery, Magenta Plains, now represents Braunig. “I think that’s what I’ve always loved: that she offers a way for the viewer to re-associate, which ends up telling them more about themselves than about Sascha.”

One of the earliest artworks in “Wrist Painter” is Sequins, from 2010. It’s from Braunig’s head series, which she began in grad school. Her process starts with a drawing, then she brings her sketch to life by making an embellished three-dimensional model. Then she’ll light the model and paint what she observes. She applies her lustrous oil paints with such precision that these strange bodily shapes start to look “real.”

She continued this system as she moved on to her bodies series a few years later, using clay resin, wire, and paper to make her maquettes. This haptic process is why works like Warm Leatherette, a 2015 painting at PMA that looks like a snatched Michelin Man encased in chartreuse padding, seem so tactile. (She has often described her choice of colors as “seasonal” or “dietary”—she finds a palette that works for one show and then cycles to a new one for the next.)

Her maquette process also lends a certain theatricality. She is literally setting up a scene, as if the entire idea of femininity were on a velvet-curtained stage, the whole specter aglow under klieg lights. What is gender if not a performance?

Braunig, who grew up in British Columbia, Canada, liked drawing and painting from a young age. Her father (a dentist) and mother (a nurse) enjoyed creative pursuits outside of work, and the house was full of art books. She loved watching Pee-wee’s Playhouse, which she credits as being just as formative, even more so, than going to museums.

Her parents encouraged her to attend an intensive summer art program at Skidmore College in upstate New York when she was 16. It was there that she first learned about Cooper Union, the New York City school where artists Lee Krasner and Wangechi Mutu got their start. Braunig decided it was the place for her.

New York City was the perfect place for the budding artist. “I was totally in love. It maybe ruined me forever,” she jokes now. She went to Yale right after Cooper, and then she moved to Maine in 2010, in her mid-20s. “I was really fortunate to have a New York gallery as I left the city, because I was able to have shows and have my work represented here even though I wasn’t here.”

Living somewhere far less expensive was also a game changer—she didn’t need to pile on jobs to make ends meet, and could instead focus on building her deeply personal style. “I had been exposed to so many influences coming to New York. And maybe those first five years in Maine were a time when I was able to process them alone.”

Braunig knows her art history, and she gets why people connect her style back to Surrealism. But her artistic comrades are more like French self-portraitist Claude Cahun or even Frida Kahlo, she says, “who’s really making work that is influenced by events of her own life.” Her art might feel dreamy (or perhaps like an acid trip), but “it’s not a dream space. I really am thinking about actual forces in the actual world.”

For many years Braunig used her work to illustrate broader ideas around culture and gender, but her most recent output has taken a turn toward the autobiographical. A few years ago she went through a creative block that left her stranded from her art practice, unable to make anything. As she emerged from this period last year, she started making her most personal paintings yet.

That work went into her radiant show “Cauldron” at Magenta Plains this past spring, its title a reference to the messy, transformative stew of midlife. Its anchor painting was My Death (2025), which, along with several others from the show, will travel to Portland for “Wrist Painter.”

Sascha Braunig, My Death, 2025, oil on linen over panel.

She calls My Death an “explicit self-portrait” about her own reanimation—an emergence through the void of creative block. It’s juicy and haunting and beautiful. She employed those tentacles again, but this time they are like a life force attached to a sleeping Braunig. A mysterious figure made from strips of vermilion orange pulls on them like marionette strings, bringing the artist back to life.

“I’ve come to understand that painting is a survival practice,” she says. “That can be a framing practice where you’re using it to talk about ideas in the world, or it can be a practice where you’re directly processing your own experiences. Maybe those two things aren’t that different.”

For the PMA show, Braunig has made one brand-new work, titled Torsion. At 78 inches tall by 60 inches wide, it is the largest painting of her career. She previously used the title for a museum show in Norway, but realized she’d never given it to an individual painting. The term, in physics, refers to the twisting of an object around an axis: a towel wrung out to dry.

This new Torsion is a painting of a fleshy pink figure with a tiny, twisted torso standing on tiptoes within a gray box. The figure’s muscular back is to the viewer, its knees fanning out in a plié. Dramatic tentacles in orange and yellow trace its exaggerated outline.

The figure’s head is cut off by the box’s ceiling, and its arms stop around the elbows, where they encounter the side walls. Maybe the figure’s been blocked, held back by the confines of this shadowy encasement. Or maybe, somehow, it has broken free.

“Sascha Braunig: Wrist Painter” is on view at the Portland Art Museum in Maine from November 13, 2026, through March 14, 2027.

NiCE’s next-generation Cognigy bets the hard part of AI agents is running them, not building them

📅 Nov 14, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

GUEST COLUMN by Zeus Kerravala

The contact center has been the low-hanging fruit for the artificial intelligence industry. Ask any information technology or business leader what they want to use agentic AI for, and they will say it’s to improve the customer experience, which starts with the contact center.

However, to date, the industry has spent the past two years arguing over which AI agent is the smartest. That’s the wrong debate. Model capabilities are improving so quickly and are getting so much cheaper that raw intelligence and reasoning are becoming the least differentiated part of the stack.

The real challenge for enterprises is operating a fleet of AI agents reliably across complete customer journeys, with the visibility and control a large business requires. Without that, the human, or worse, the customer, is left connecting the dots among the outputs of the growing number of agents they are communicating with.

That’s the thesis behind the next generation of NiCE Cognigy, which NiCE Ltd. launched this week. The release includes a redesigned Agentic Experience Studio, an AI Assist companion spanning the building, testing and improvement of agents, reusable “skills” that package instructions and tools, turn-by-turn tracing and native voice-to-voice model support.

Default speech-to-text, text-to-speech and large language models are now included in the per-conversation price, with premium models from Deepgram, ElevenLabs and Anthropic available with a few clicks and billed by usage.

Ahead of the launch, I attended an analyst briefing with Nhu Ho, senior product marketing manager at NiCE Cognigy, and with Clement Boutier, director of product management, who leads the team that built the release and owns the platform roadmap.

Here are the important points from this release:

Ho opened with a chart showing how long AI can work on its own before a human has to step in. In 2020, it was seconds; in 2024, it was minutes; and this year it’s roughly 16 hours. The footnote was interesting, as it calculated that figure at a 50% success rate. “Capable and dependable do not always mean the same thing,” Ho said. “That is exactly where the platform matters.”

For CX leaders, that’s a solid benchmark to consider. A 50% success rate is a research milestone. In a contact center, it’s a churn event or a compliance violation. As models commoditize, value shifts to the system around them: testing, tracing, guardrails and deterministic fallbacks. NiCE doesn’t build frontier models, and frontier model builders don’t run contact centers. Positioning Cognigy as the layer that makes AI dependable is a smart place to plant a flag.

Cognigy earned its pre-acquisition leadership position by handling complex production deployments for large brands such as Lufthansa, Mercedes and DHL. But that flexibility and customizability added complexity. Boutier put it this way: “The large number of options when configuring an agent creates friction to get started, which slows things down.” The new studio lets a builder stand up an agent in a few clicks, with models already provisioned, while power-user tools, including full access to the agent’s system prompt, are available in advanced sections.

This is important because NiCE aims to create a “1+1=3” value proposition for its current customers. NiCE needs Cognigy to expand broadly across the CXone installed base, not just among expert teams at the largest enterprises. Boutier described three builder personas: smaller businesses building simple agents, contact center administrators and team leads, and engineers at technically sophisticated companies. AI Assist is aimed squarely at the first two.

In the demo, it built an agent, tested it, fixed the problems it found, and deployed it to WhatsApp. The flip side is that lowering the barrier means more agents built by less experienced people, which makes the governance features the real story of this release.

Despite all the agentic hype, most enterprises aren’t ready to hand over entire journeys to an autonomous agent. Boutier outlined three modes: traditional natural language understanding, hybrid and fully agentic. Hybrid “is actually where most of our customers are,” he said, while fully agentic deployments are at the “beginning of the adoption curve.” This approach lets customers ease into agentic, build confidence and move to fully autonomous at their own pace.

NiCE designed the product for that approach. An agent can serve as the orchestration layer, calling existing flows as tools. Flows can also be placed in front of agents, for example, to scrub credit card numbers or other personal data before any data reaches a model. Ho described the release as “an evolution, not a replacement,” with production deployments, application programming interfaces and integrations continuing to run unchanged.

That’s a meaningful advantage over AI-native challengers whose pitch effectively requires a rebuild. Contact centers are the fastest-moving environments, and deterministic workflows aren’t legacy baggage. They’re how a business enforces what a large language model shouldn’t be deciding.

“You can have the best builder experience, but if you don’t have any feedback loop on what you’re building, you’re never going to build a great agent,” Boutier said. The new tracing shows each turn: which skill loaded, which tool was called and with what arguments, and when the agent handed off to a flow.

The Simulator now supports multivariate testing of up to 10 variants, using an LLM-as-judge to run synthetic customers against an agent. Boutier ran the same agent on two models and found that one was faster and consumed fewer tokens. This shifts model selection from a preference to a data-backed economic decision.

Voice-to-voice support may be the first feature customers deploy. Boutier noted that buyers are open to using a speech-to-text and text-to-speech cascade, “but what they realize is that voice-to-voice models sound just better.” In the demo, the agent delegated tool calls to a separate model while continuing to talk, making the conversation sound far more natural.

Cognigy now serves as both a Model Context Protocol client and server and supports agent-to-agent connections, including one customer linking Cognigy to agents built with Microsoft’s tooling. NiCE is also co-developing the Personal Agent Protocol with Meta and Sierra, which isn’t live yet. Boutier described Cognigy as “the front door” of the contact center, where a consumer’s personal agent must authenticate before it can proceed. It’s great to see NiCE collaborating with Sierra, a direct competitor, on this, as it makes things easier for a customer.

The industry views agent identity as a shared responsibility. But as Sierra outlines, companies can also let personal agents work through their websites and APIs, so the front-door position depends on enterprises routing that traffic through Cognigy. This lets brands interact with personal agents safely.

There has been a wave of companies banning personal agents, which I think is a mistake. If something is good for a consumer, it will happen, so it’s better to be on the leading edge of enabling it rather than fighting it.

This release is less about new capabilities and more about NiCE betting on where value will accrue as AI agents mature. Intelligence is getting cheaper. Dependability, governance and the ability to blend autonomous reasoning with deterministic control are not.

NiCE is the market leader in contact center as a service, as Cognigy was in conversational AI. Now the challenge is to get an easier-to-work-with Cognigy into the hands of thousands of CXone customers without creating a sprawl of individually governed agents. This will allow NiCE to maintain its industry leadership as personal agents arrive. As it does that, the Cognigy acquisition will move from an add-on to a core component of the NiCE platform.

Zeus Kerravala is a principal analyst at ZK Research, a division of Kerravala Consulting. He wrote this article for SiliconANGLE.

Starship flight 15 powers to orbit—setting up a possible catch on Saturday

📅 Nov 14, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

One way or another, the company will get some more data on Saturday.

STARBASE, Texas—Starship lifted off right on schedule Friday from South Texas, making its 15th test flight in more than three years.

The largely successful launch took place in the early evening; as the massive rocket rose into the atmosphere, the Sun sank toward the western horizon. Conditions were ideal for the launch, with the atmospheric winds so calm the rocket’s white plume did not dissipate for many minutes. The weather in South Texas is not always comfortable; summers are long and hot. Friday evening was perfect at Starbase.

The first stage ascended toward space flawlessly, releasing the upper stage above the Gulf of Mexico. From there, the Starship upper stage ignited its Raptor engines and powered its way into a nominal orbit. This was the second time SpaceX has flown Starship into low-Earth orbit.

When it came time for the first stage to return to the ground, it dropped like a rock. However, due to an unspecified issue, the vehicle was commanded to divert into the Gulf rather than return to the Starbase facility to be caught by the launch tower. It created a fantastically powerful explosion, even for observers more than 10 miles away.

The primary aim of Friday’s launch was the deployment of 26 Starlink V3 satellites from a narrow door at the base of the spacecraft. This occurred about 40 minutes into Starship’s flight, and all 26 satellites—which have solar panels that stretch 60 meters end to end when fully deployed—safely made it into orbit, where they will now use onboard propulsion to raise their altitude.

This means that Starship is now an operational, orbital rocket. It is the largest and most powerful machine humans have ever launched into space. The challenge for the company now is to make Starship reusable and dramatically increase its flight rate.

In that sense, the first part of Friday’s mission was not a success. SpaceX had hoped to return the first-stage booster, refurbish it, and fly it again. The company has previously landed and reflown Starship boosters but has yet to do so with the latest iteration of the vehicle, version 3.

However, SpaceX still has a chance to make history on Saturday with the potential return of the Starship upper stage. SpaceX has never attempted to catch the vehicle’s upper stage.

Doing so will require Starship to pass a series of tests in orbit and subsequently during its return to Earth to ensure it is fully healthy. This is critical because Starship will be passing over parts of Mexico and the United States before returning to Starbase, and SpaceX does not want to endanger people on the ground. Additionally, it does not want to risk damage to its launch towers.

If Starship passes these health checks, it would land at about 3:15 pm local time in Texas (21:15 UTC). If it does not, the company has earlier options to de-orbit the vehicle into an ocean.

Successfully returning Starship’s upper stage would be historic, as well as significant for SpaceX’s efforts to reuse the massive rocket. It would also give the company valuable information about the vehicle’s performance during reentry.

One of the biggest hurdles left in Starship’s development is the vehicle’s heat shield, which must protect the upper stage during its return through Earth’s atmosphere. More than that, SpaceX aims to make Starship rapidly reusable, and to do this, Starship’s tiles must be light and resilient.

SpaceX has been experimenting with tiles since the beginning of the Starship program, trying to find the right combination of low mass, heat resistance, and durability so the tiles require minimal refurbishment between launches. For this flight, SpaceX said it is applying learnings from the previous test flight in September, including additional retention mechanisms on tiles in areas deemed to be at the highest risk of falling off during ascent and addressing areas with a flow path for plasma to get behind the tiles.

Another problem on recent flights has been heat incursion into the small gaps between the edges of the tiles. According to SpaceX, this Starship has a large portion of the heat shield equipped with a curved tile design that has shown the ability to reduce heating in the gaps between tiles.

Ars Technica has been separating the signal from the noise for over 25 years. With our unique combination of technical savvy and wide-ranging interest in the technological arts and sciences, Ars is the trusted source in a sea of information. After all, you don’t need to know everything, only what’s important.

Netflix's 'The Diplomat' Already Has the Perfect Ending Hiding in Plain Sight

📅 Nov 14, 2026  |  Category: Politics  |  Read Source →

Netflix's The Diplomat is still on viewers' minds following the release of its fourth season. The political thriller has become one of the streamer's most consistent shows, with major twists and complex relationships defining each season. Over the course of the series so far, the tense political situation Kate (Keri Russell), her husband Hal (Rufus Sewell), and their allies face has only worsened and can't be easily resolved, but the story already seems to be heading toward a natural endpoint.

Wrapping up a series is notoriously difficult, especially for a show like The Diplomat that thrives on surprising its audience. However, its most recent season serves as an example of what a rewarding series finale could look like through Kate and Hal's unexpected choices. With the show now confirmed to return for Season 5, The Diplomat is already pushing its characters toward the ending they deserve.

The Diplomat has had no shortage of political scandal, but the series is too realistic to expect two characters to correct the entire system overnight. While the larger story may not be easily wrapped up before the end, Kate and Hal can still earn satisfying character arcs. Over the first four seasons, Kate and Hal have grown, both professionally and in their relationship, and a good ending has to highlight that evolution. Season 4's finale moves in the right direction when Kate steps down from her position. In doing so, she chooses the moral good over the political hierarchy, a struggle she's grappled with since the beginning of the series.

Season 4 does the same for Hal's journey as he willingly lets go of the power he holds as Vice President. It's a huge development for his character as he goes from refusing to take a back seat during Kate's time as ambassador to following Kate's lead over his own self-interest. This decision is especially shocking since both characters have repeatedly prioritized their careers, but that's exactly why the twist is so effective. Season 4's finale proves that Kate sticking to her principles in the messy world of politics is exactly what The Diplomat needs to embrace in its inevitable series finale.

While Season 4's shocking cliffhanger gives viewers a taste of what the end could look like, with Kate and Hal's bold choices catapulting The Diplomat into a new era, the story isn't over just yet. Little is confirmed about Season 5, but Netflix announced a renewal soon after Season 4 aired, and if the release pattern of the previous seasons continues, the series should return in Fall 2027. There's also no indication Season 5 will be the last, giving The Diplomat plenty of time to explore the ramifications of Kate and Hal's decision and reinforce their growth.

Four Netflix characters, four completely different ways of showing up for somebody. This is the one who fits how you actually do friendship, and what having them in your life would be like.

You do not do friendship at half volume, and neither does he. What you want is somebody who says yes to the plan before you have finished describing the plan.

You are the friend who makes things happen, and so is she. Together you would be mildly exhausting to everyone around you and completely delighted with yourselves.

You have no patience for pleasant lies, and she has no ability to tell them. What looks like coldness from the outside is the most reliable friendship on this list.

You look after whoever is having the worst day. So does he. It is the least strategic way to move through the world and the best possible quality in a friend.

Overall, The Diplomat Season 4 is moving the series in the right direction. The next installment will likely explore the fallout of the finale's twist, forcing the Wylers to question their recent choices and incorporating the very growth that the show itself depends on to thrive. Kate and Hal are facing a huge challenge as their public resignations and Grace Penn's (Allison Janney) fury could lead to a major political scandal, but to reach a truly satisfying ending, the series must explore more inner conflict. If The Diplomat leans into that story, it has the perfect opportunity to highlight Kate and Hal's long-term character development while setting up the ultimate ending the show deserves.

'MobLand' Star's New Crime Thriller Officially Removed From Over 800 Theaters

📅 Nov 14, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Helen Mirren has spent decades proving that she can make just about any character compelling, whether she's playing royalty in The Queen or a ruthless crime boss in Paramount+'s MobLand. The Oscar-winning actress has also continued taking on challenging roles well into her eighties, with her latest movie transforming her into one of literature's most fascinating and controversial figures. Unfortunately, the theatrical numbers on her newest movie tell a very different story.

Mirren's new thriller A Talent for Murder has officially lost 842 theaters since its October 23 debut, shrinking from 876 locations to just 34 after earning approximately $1.13 million domestically. The film opened to $717,864 across 876 theaters before suffering a sharp decline in its second weekend, when its theater count fell to 332 and ticket sales dropped 79% to $152,296.

Things deteriorated further during its third weekend, with another 298 theaters dropping the movie and leaving it playing in only 34 locations. That represents a staggering 96% reduction from its opening footprint, despite the film receiving generally favorable reviews.

Directed by Anton Corbijn, A Talent for Murder follows celebrated novelist Patricia Highsmith, the creator of The Talented Mr. Ripley, during her retirement in the Swiss Alps. In the movie, Edward Ridgeway is sent by his employer, a publishing house, to see reclusive author Patricia Highsmith.

It's 1995, and Ridgeway has been tasked with getting Highsmith to sign a contract promising a sixth Tom Ripley book. On the train there, he meets Clementine Balfour, who's on her way to visit her wealthy aunt. When Ridgeway meets Highsmith, sparks fly: She alternately toys with him and threatens to throw him out. When she teasingly begins outlining the new Ripley story, she asks him for a character name. He volunteers the name "Clementine Balfour," and then viewers start to see the new story unfold, as a new Ripley romances Clementine, with his eye on the family fortune. But soon fiction becomes dangerously entwined with life.

The film stars Mirren (The Queen) as Patricia Highsmith, Alden Ehrenreich (Oppenheimer) as Edward Ridgeway, Olivia Cooke (House of the Dragon) as Clementine Balfour, Juliet Stevenson (Bend It Like Beckham) as Peggy, and James Faulkner (Game of Thrones) as Douglas Balfour.

A Talent for Murder opened in US theaters on October 23, 2026, and is now available through digital rental and purchase platforms. Stay tuned to Collider for all the latest news and updates on the biggest and best movies and shows heading to your favorite streaming platforms soon.

Rock & Roll Hall of Fame Induction Ceremony to Proceed as Planned Despite FAA Grounding Flights Nationwide

📅 Nov 14, 2026  |  Category: Sports  |  Read Source →

All of the artists set to participate in Saturday's event are already in Los Angeles.

The Rock & Roll Hall of Fame induction ceremony is expected to proceed as planned tomorrow at the Peacock Theater in Los Angeles despite the Federal Aviation Administration grounding all flights nationwide Friday afternoon. All of the artists taking part in the ceremony are already in town, a representative for the Rock Hall confirmed to The Hollywood Reporter.

“We’ve been in rehearsals for two days, and the show is going to be incredible and not to be missed,” the representative said.

This year’s induction class for the performer categories include Phil Collins, Wu-Tang Clan, Oasis, Billy Idol, Luther Vandross, Sade, Joy Division/New Order and Iron Maiden. Oasis, Sade and Iron Maiden had already confirmed earlier this week that they wouldn’t be attending. Related Stories TV 'Dancing With the Stars' Rock & Roll Hall of Fame Night Ends With Gloomy Elimination and One Perfect Score: See the Scores, Who Went Home Music Olivia Rodrigo, Doja Cat, Elton John Among Initial Rock Hall Induction Special Guests

Queen Latifah, Gram Parsons, Celia Cruz Fela Kuti and MC Lyte will all be honored with the early influence award, while Linda Creed, Arif Mardin, Jimmy Miller and Rick Rubin will receive the musical excellence award. Dick Van Dyke will be honored with the Ahmet Ertegun award, which is reserved for non-performing industry figures.

While the Rock Hall managed to avoid any issue, the grounding could cause major headaches in the entertainment world if it extends through tomorrow; the Film Academy’s star-studded Governors Awards, for example, are scheduled to take place on Sunday. Meanwhile, an extended disruption could presumably impact concerts if artists need to travel by plane to get to their venues. ESPN reported Friday that the ground stop has disrupted air travel for several college football teams for their games this weekend.

The Difficulty of Twin Estrangement

📅 Nov 14, 2026  |  Category: World  |  Read Source →

After years or decades, problems and patterns can become deeply entrenched. Be a fly on the wall during a sample couples therapy session and witness the slow, deliberate work of changing those patterns for a more enduring love.

Self Tests are all about you. Are you outgoing or introverted? Are you a narcissist? Does perfectionism hold you back? Find out the answers to these questions and more with Psychology Today.

Posted November 13, 2026 | Reviewed by Monica Vilhauer Ph.D.

Twin estrangement is a really important issue that is often ignored because twin mythology suggests that all twins should get along, as their closeness creates a perfect interpersonal relationship. Raising twins is hard, and estrangement is very predictable when there is not enough focus on individual development. Believing that all twins should and will be able to get along causes shame and humiliation for twins and their parents when they don't. This is an unfortunate problem.

For most of my adult life, I have been writing about twins or gifted children. In general, my focus is on the social and emotional problems related to identity development that twins or other types of multiple births naturally experience because they share their caregivers and home environment. Twin development, in general, is quite different and more complicated than the developmental identity challenges of non-twins. The most common problems that twins have when they are young are questions and concerns that revolve around the sharing of their parents, day-to-day experiences, friendships, and education. Twins also have concerns about their twin's opinion, and their need for their twin to be present in stressful situations and understand immediately what is wrong.

Specific conflicts or pressures that lead to downright unhappiness, intense arguments, or confusion with life without their twin include the following:

The most common and unfortunate disagreements occur when twins continue to be estranged during the holiday season and cannot get the support they need from their twin. Twin estrangement is often seen when one twin is favored over the other by their parents or other caregiving adults.

Social development, including making relationships with non-twins, is also unique and very complicated as twins develop and mature. I can say with great certainty that fitting into the non-twin world is enormously difficult for most twins, who feel ashamed of themselves for fighting with their twin sister or brother and at the same time lonely for their twin sister or brother’s companionship and advice. Outsiders directly question why a twin cannot get along with their truest companion. These questions about closeness frustrate and annoy twins who are trying to get along and falling short of doing so.

Estrangement can grow out of frustration when twins disagree. When differences can be reconciled, the harmony between twins can be reestablished, and estrangement will be reduced. Twin estrangement is both very serious and hard to control. Do not minimize or ignore estrangement. Parenting for individuality helps twins get over estrangement more easily than twins who are encouraged to compete with each other and stay alike.

My interest in twins and their unique development is related to my own identity as a twin and why my twin and I could not resolve our love-hate relationship. As a USC graduate student in psychology and education, I learned a great deal about how twins were and are still used in psychological research to understand the relative importance of nature, nurture, and parenting in development. When genetic determinants are important, twins are often used to figure out what is inherited and what is a function of proper environmental care. There is no agreement about what is more important, genes or environmental care, for identity development, but there is a lot of research on this issue. In my opinion, all of these influences are extremely critical to twin identity development. In particular, parental attentiveness is extremely valuable.

Taking care of twins is extremely time-consuming and difficult physically, emotionally, and personally. Finding help and paying the bills for all of the extras is a challenge. Here are some of my suggestions for reducing your parenting stress levels.

1. Make sure that you get to know each of your twins' likes and dislikes. Know your twins as individuals with unique interests and feelings.

2. Teach twins how to get along with each other, even if this seems impossible when they are fighting intensely or arguing with you or each other.

3. Make sure that your twins know that some toys, food, attention, friends, and interests cannot be shared. This point of view will promote individuality and reduce anger, fighting, and estrangement.

4. Help your twins understand that friendships should not be shared.

5. Find twin families that want to be your friends and arrange family or twin play dates.

It is hard to be a twin, and it is hard to raise twins. Try to get the best help you can. Educate yourself on what twins need to thrive.

Barbara Klein, Ph.D., Ed.D., is an author and educational consultant who has done extensive research on the development of twin identity.

Epic's Tim Sweeney says Steam's AI rule is "unfair to developers who use AI to make good games"

📅 Nov 14, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Join the GamesRadar community for quick access. Enter your email below and we'll send confirmation, and sign you up to our newsletter.

Keep exploring and earning more as a member.

From the creators of Edge: A weekly videogame industry newsletter with analysis from expert writers, guidance from professionals, and insight into what's on the horizon.

Get sneak previews, exclusive competitions and details of special events each month!

"It's a guilt by association pattern that all developers need to push back on"

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Epic Games CEO Tim Sweeney has been in a bit of a one-sided beef with Valve co-founder and CEO Gabe Newell for a while now, lately about Steam's AI disclosure that requires developers to include a label informing Steam users of AI-generated content. Sweeney has had a number of complaints about Valve's storefront over the years, which makes sense as he owns one of its main competitors, the Epic Games Store, but he's been doubling and tripling down on the AI disclosure issue as of late. In a new interview, he not only sharply rebukes the AI label, but embraces AI in game development to degree rarely seen by him or other industry leaders.

Talking to GamesBeat, Sweeney gives one of the most bullish takes on AI generation in games I've seen in my many years covering this topic. Get on-board or die, is essentially his perspective.

"It's really clear to me that large-scale game developers–setting aside indies, where there's always going to be specialties and niches, large-scale game development will be totally changed by AI," Sweeney says. "Teams that don't adopt it will absolutely be left behind and rendered uneconomical, non-viable compared to their peers."

Sweeney says he's against any kind of legislation to curb the use of AI in game development in the US because it would give other countries the advantage. "Everyone is going to be adopting it," he says. "When one team can make a game four times more efficiently – 10 times more efficiently soon, perhaps – than another team can make the same game, there's just no possibility of competing the old way. No game developer has 75% to 95% profit margins that they can put at risk by not being as productive as possible."

There has been massive backlash to AI in games from players, voice actors, artists, and indie developers, but messaging from big studios and publishers generally ranges from cautiously optimistic to vocally and enthusiastically supportive. Meanwhile, a report from GDC found that 55% of respondents said AI was having a negative impact on the industry. Still, Sweeney wants to make sure companies aren't being bullied into choosing human workers over AI.

"Because there's a big resistance movement against AI that seems concentrated on trying to bully developers into not adopting it by attacking individuals, by review-bombing projects and so on, I've been trying to make my views known to as many game developers as possible," he says. "We have to power through this and build awesome games, use maximum acceleration, because it is a survival issue for all the companies in the industry."

Sweeney has called Valve's AI policy "abhorrent," politically motivated, and nonsensical among other things, and we can add another descriptor to the pile today: toxic. When GamesBeat pushes back and suggests Steam's AI label is there to inform players, Sweeney says he doesn't "buy it in the slightest," pointing to Steam's user review system as a means to let players know about "a game's quality, scope, entertainment value and so on." Ultimately, he says AI can be a force for good and bad in game development, and he sees Valve's mandatory disclosure as a judgment on the technology as a whole.

Weekly digests, tales from the communities you love, and more

"You can build good games and bad games without AI. You can build good games and bad games with AI. You can repeat those distinctions for all engines, all development tools, all methodologies, all countries of origin, all teams and so on," says Sweeney. "If we were to identify AI as associated with bad games, that's just unfair to developers who use AI to make good games. It's a guilt by association pattern that all developers need to push back on."

Sweeney argues that Steam's AI disclosure is "being used toxically here, because it's absolutely being used by anti-AI advocates to undermine the reputations of developers and the viability of their projects."

Newell himself has yet to respond to Sweeney's many tirades, but prominent former Valve developer Chet Faliszek hit back in a YouTube video in which he called Sweeney's behavior "AI psychosis." Faliszek also argued that Sweeney was "treating all the players like children" and advised him to "act as a player yourself for a moment and go, 'What would I want to know about a game?'"

Why so many game developers don't want to use generative AI

I got my big break here in 2019 with a freelance news gig, and I was hired on as GamesRadar's west coast Staff Writer in 2021. That means I'm responsible for managing the site's western regional executive branch, AKA my home office, and writing about whatever horror game I'm too afraid to finish.

HeartFlow: 3 Key Takeaways for Long-Term Investors

📅 Nov 14, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Operating losses are narrowing as revenue outpaces expenses, and the company's $253 million cash pile is meant to carry it until it turns cash-flow positive.

On its third-quarter 2026 earnings call, held on Nov. 12, 2026, HeartFlow (HTFL -19.13%) raised its full-year revenue outlook to between $262 million and $264 million. The company is accelerating commercial adoption of its artificial intelligence diagnostic platform across cardiovascular care pathways.

Expanding adoption and clinical trial milestones offer long-term investors a clear view of sustainable top-line growth. Three things management said on the call stand out.

Revenue grew 57% year over year in the third quarter of 2026, driven by higher utilization of FFRct, an artificial intelligence tool measuring coronary artery blockage, and Plaque Analysis, a software program analyzing arterial vessel walls. Expanding sales of high-margin software helped lift non-GAAP gross margin, an adjusted measure excluding non-cash compensation, to 83.8%.

A one-year study of 6,000 patients revealed that clinicians using Plaque Analysis reduced major cardiovascular disease (CVD) events by 34% compared to standard coronary computed tomography angiography, or CCTA, scans alone. Demonstrating improved patient outcomes encourages health systems to adopt the software across a broader portion of scan volumes.

Management is initiating three clinical trials in late 2026 and early 2027 to evaluate high-risk patients without symptoms, aiming to unlock an additional $6 billion market. Winning regulatory and reimbursement coverage in this population would expand the company's total addressable market, or TAM, to $11 billion.

Operational leverage continues to strengthen as revenue growth outpaces expenses, narrowing operating losses significantly. The company holds $253 million in cash and short-term investments, which management expects will fund operations until achieving positive cash flow.

Structural advantages in proprietary imaging software, growing clinical trial evidence, and expanding health plan coverage protect its competitive position as management focuses on account activations and pricing discipline.

New products like PCI Navigator, a software tool for planning catheter-based heart stent procedures, extend the platform into treatment planning. Clinical data supporting these tools helps embed the software into hospital workflows and drives long-term customer retention.

James Gunn Says “No One From Skydance Has Seen” ‘Man Of Tomorrow’: “There Are A Lot Of Untrue Rumors Going Around”

📅 Nov 14, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

James Gunn is clearing up some rumors following his exit as co-head of DC Studios.

With Skydance making changes amid the Paramount and Warner Bros. merger, Peter Safran and Gunn announced they would not be heading DC Studios.

Amid their exits, many rumors have circulated. One prominent story was that Skydance saw the Superman sequel, titled Man of Tomorrow, and planned to make some edits, but Gunn has shut down those allegations.

“There are a lot of untrue rumors going around, but one of them is that Skydance has seen Man of Tomorrow and that all sorts of sweeping changes are being made,” Gunn posted on Threads.

He continued, “No one from Skydance has even seen the movie – the only people that have are Peter Safran and a very small trusted group of friends and family. So any stories around this narrative are false.” Watch on Deadline

Man of Tomorrow wrapped filming in August, with Gunn sharing a joyful post and celebrating everyone involved in the project.

“That’s a wrap on principal photography for Man of Tomorrow,” Gunn shared on Threads. “Truly a joyful experience, and I have immense gratitude to my cast and crew.”

Amid the executive shakeup at Skydance, former Marvel exec Jeremy Latcham is now set to run DC Studios.

Gov. Ferguson seeks $10M to help develop Eastern Wash. clean-energy hub

📅 Nov 14, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Challenges, breakthroughs and big ideas in the race to protect our planet. SEE MORE

by Lisa Stiffler on Nov 13, 2026 at 4:32 pmNovember 13, 2026 at 4:32 pm

Gov. Bob Ferguson is proposing $10 million to fund basic infrastructure needed for the Northwest Advanced Clean Energy Park, a master-planned industrial area in Eastern Washington that will foster development of large-scale clean energy, advanced manufacturing and supply chains. The proposal will be included in his 2027–29 capital budget.

If approved by lawmakers, the funding would support sewer, water, electrical and road infrastructure at the park.

“This investment will support thousands of good-paying jobs in the trades and advanced industries, and unlock billions in private investment,” Ferguson said in a statement.

The 1,641-acre site was formerly part of the Hanford Nuclear Reservation, a sprawling federal property along the Columbia River near the Tri-Cities that is undergoing a massive environmental cleanup. Hanford was established during World War II to produce plutonium for the Manhattan Project.

According to the governor’s office, two projects have committed to locating at the park once infrastructure construction is complete: Atlas Agro’s low-carbon fertilizer production plant and the utility-scale Ruby Flats Solar Farm.

State officials said General Matter is also considering the location for producing advanced nuclear fuel for small reactors. That siting could potentially be beneficial for another project planned for the same area: Amazon, X-energy and Energy Northwest are developing plans for the Cascade Advanced Energy Facility, a nuclear plant comprised of clustered, small reactors.

In 2024, the Norwegian company Cenate expressed interest in building a factory at the park to produce lithium-ion battery components, according to the Tri-Cities Area Journal of Business, ultimately withdrew its plans.

The Tri-Cities is also attracting fusion-energy investment. Last year, Seattle startup Avalanche Energy landed a $10 million grant from the Washington State Department of Commerce’s Green Jobs Grant Program to develop FusionWERX, a commercial-scale testing facility for fusion technologies. The project is being developed in an existing Port of Benton building in Richland.

Washington is heading into its 2027–29 biennial budget with a multibillion-dollar shortfall, but officials have not yet settled on a definitive public estimate.

Ferguson has acknowledged the scale of the gap but has not publicly specified an amount. The shortfall concerns the state’s operating budget, which is separate from the capital budget funding proposed for the energy park.

The Climate Pledge is a commitment to reach net-zero carbon emissions by 2040, co-founded by Amazon and Global Optimism in 2019.

Seven years later, the community has grown to over 700 signatories across 62 industries and 49 countries and territories, reducing emissions, scaling emerging climate solutions, and addressing challenges across operations and value chains.

I Was Surprised By Daniel Radcliffe's Take On Giving Harry Potter Advice

📅 Nov 14, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Join The Club for quick access. Enter your email below and we'll send confirmation, and sign you up to our newsletter.

Daniel Radcliffe And Keira Knightley Have The Same Take On Fans Quoting Their Movies At Them

After Dancing With The Stars Disney Night, I Have Complaints About Jenna And Harry

Rupert Grint Reveals His ‘Hope’ For The New Ron Weasley (And I’m Getting Emotional)

Unlock instant access to exclusive member features.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

With the new Harry Potter series getting closer, Daniel Radcliffe has been getting a lot of questions about any advice he has for Dominic McLaughlin. The actor has previously said he didn’t have advice for McLaughlin and only shared advice for the producers. At the time, he asked them to “let [the young cast] be kids still,” especially in the age of social media. However, I was surprised by his take on why he doesn’t want to give advice.

While the actor was promoting his upcoming movie, Steps, he was asked by AP News Entertainment about handing down the wand. The actor doubled down on not sharing advice, but this time he explained why:

I’m really, truly in the business of not giving Dominic advice. He’s got it, he doesn’t need any advice from me. I don’t want to be like a — because then if I say something to you, then the next interview [with him], people will be like, ‘Oh, Dan said you should do this.’ And I don’t want to haunt this poor child through interviews.

It’s understandable that he doesn’t want to put even more on McLaughlin’s shoulders. It’s a little surprising, though I can see why he doesn’t want to put the young actor under pressure to live up to anything. Meanwhile, he’s not the only Harry Potter actor who has been asked to hand down advice. Bonnie Wright, who played Ginny Weasley, shares a similar opinion to Radcliffe when it comes to telling the next Ginny what to do. She has stated, “I think it’s so important for them to be in their world and their interpretation of it.”

This seems to be largely what the original cast thinks, with Tom Felton also veering away from giving advice on how to portray Draco. However, the Broadway actor focused on the sentimentality of his Harry Potter days and advises the new cast to take lots of pictures. It seems that Radcliffe isn’t the only one not wishing to ‘haunt’ the child actors. Though this take on advice is a little surprising, Radcliffe’s reveal that he’s not a big TV watcher is even more so:

I don’t know if I will [watch] honestly. I’ve been in a lot of Harry Potter already and also, not even because I don’t — I don’t watch any TV. I haven’t seen Breaking Bad, and I haven’t seen Mad Men. I haven’t seen some of the real classics. Eight hours is a lot of anything to watch.

I’m not surprised he hasn’t watched his own franchise, but he’s so behind on so many classics. Perhaps he’ll check out the Harry Potter series with his son. Hopefully, after Season 1 airs, someone will ask him. Though I doubt watching the series will change his stance on giving advice.

Meanwhile, this hasn’t stopped a few of his co-stars. Ralph Fiennes gave some practical advice to whoever takes his place as Voldemort: “Practice your long, flowing, robe walk” and advised him not to trip. Additionally, a pair of Weasleys have shared the sweet gesture they gave to the next Fred and George, up-and-coming actors Tristan and Gabriel Harland. Oliver and James Phelps sent the twins sweet notes wishing the next set of mischief makers the best of luck. They declined to give exact details about what was in the notes, stating they were private messages of encouragement.

The Harry Potter series will arrive on the TV schedule on Christmas Day. It is expected to have a weekly release and will be available with an HBO Max subscription.

GQ’s 2026 Men of the Year Party Brought Everyone Out

📅 Nov 14, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

There’s no party quite like a GQ party. Who else could bring together Andrew Garfield, Robby Hoffman, Future, and Angel Reese under one roof? But that’s exactly what we did on Thursday night at Los Angeles’s famed Chateau Marmont for our 31st Men of the Year party, presented by Johnnie Walker Blue Label.

Following a dinner honoring our trio of cover stars—Matt Damon, Chris Rock, and Garfield—the real fun began when all three of them, along with nearly every other celebrity in town, descended on the Chateau’s garden to mix it up to the sounds of DJ Yung Tada (plus a late-night set from GQ Breakouts electro-pop duo Fcukers).

I caught Damon at the entrance to the party looking to continue our conversation from his cover story, but our reunion was cut short when Inde Navarette pulled up to say hi and exchange contacts. If the 25-year-old Obsession newcomer was starstruck by the 56-year-old Hollywood hero, the feeling seemed mutual.

Future and Travis Scott rolled up nearly at the same time; Travis, who performed the end-credits track for The Odyssey, told me he was ready to hit the Oscars campaign trail.

In every corner was a cluster of stars from different Hollywood galaxies. Abbott Elementary’s Tyler James Williams and Chris Perfetti made the rounds together, looking nothing like the harried teachers they play on TV. GQ Breakout Owen Thiele and his longtime friend Cazzie David (who gave Owen his first acting gig on her web series years ago) ignored our red carpet instructions to bless us with photogenic poses they’d pre-planned long in advance. When I asked broadcaster Taylor Rooks for an on-the-fly NBA Cup prediction, she gushed about the Celtics and the Lakers. And there was something fun about seeing Anderson .Paak and Diplo chop it up, too.

The friendly embrace shared by Austin Abrams and Skyler Gisondo, noted Good Dudes, is the unscripted team-up the internet goes nuts for. The Resident Evil and Primetime actors later shared that they’d just been on the phone together while getting ready for the party, neither realizing they’d be seeing each other shortly. When I brought up James Gunn and his recent departure from DC Studios, Gisondo called it a “bummer”—he just wrapped the “Jimmy Olsen vs. Gorilla Grodd” series, he said, and hopes that it’ll still come out (and that Gunn continues to write and direct even if he’s not in executive mode anymore).

In other unlikely but endearing encounters: Ayo Edebiri and Thiele excitedly nabbed IShowSpeed for a photo, and Lamorne Morris stopped Anthony Ippolito to give him props for I Play Rocky, counseling him to take it all in. Ippolito told me he’s headed off to Europe soon to continue promoting the film; a certified foodie, he was hunting for Paris recs (I had to put him on to HolyBelly, for the pancakes). I also caught GQ’s Director of the Year Denis Villeneuve, whose eyes lit up when I mentioned his woefully underrated Jake Gyllenhaal thriller Enemy.

Midway through the night I spotted Andrew Garfield and Monica Barbaro, who were considering an Irish exit. Garfield’s forthcoming Artificial (out December 25) is the movie on everybody’s lips, but he told me he’s looking forward to spending the holidays in New York and taking a brief break before what will probably be a busy campaign season for the Man of the Year.

No one captured the evening as succinctly as Robby Hoffman, who described her upcoming TV show as “Curb [Your Enthusiasm] for poor people.” When the conversation turned back to GQ, Hoffman rued the decline of magazine paper quality. But this latest issue got her seal of approval: appropriately glossy, well-considered, and elite to the touch. Though she might have just been describing the party’s guest list.

But even if you didn’t make the cut, you can see how GQ’s Men of the Year 2026 party looked from the inside. ✦

HBO’s 5-Part Crime Drama Has One of the Best Seasons in TV History

📅 Nov 14, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

The Wire’s fourth season provided a far deeper look at Baltimore’s problems

The Wire’s fourth season provided a far deeper look at Baltimore’s problems

At the turn of the 21st century, HBO was the place for prestige crime drama. The launch of The Sopranos in 1999 paved the way for the 2002 premiere of The Wire, which focused on drug dealers in Baltimore and the police officers trying to disrupt their operations. The series is still one of the best shows of all time, and provided breakout roles for huge stars like Idris Elba and Michael B. Jordan. But while it was compelling from the beginning, The Wire truly achieved perfection in season 4 when the scope expanded beyond the drug trade to examine the cycle of institutional failure.

Season 1 of The Wire was tightly focused on the drug empire of Avon Barksdale (Wood Harris) and his business-savvy second-in-command Stringer Bell (Elba). Showrunner David Simon quickly established a brilliant mix of humor and pathos. The show could find comedy in something as simple as cops struggling to get new furniture into their office, and then gut audiences with the death of a relatable drug dealer. Season 2 shifted the focus to the city’s port system, a jarring change as many characters faded into the background to make room for the tragic tale of the Sobotka family. Season 3 was the most tightly linked to the show’s first season, largely completing the story of the Barksdale gang.

That made season 4 perfect for a soft reboot, a shift that was defined by star Dominic West all but disappearing after his relentless detective, Jimmy McNulty, was demoted for pissing off his bosses too many times. The detectives he previously worked with in the major crimes unit continue their work, investigating a vicious new gang that’s filling the void Barksdale left behind. Lester Freamon (Clarke Peters) effectively steps into the McNulty role of pursuing the truth, even when his supervisors would love for him to be a little less dogged, bringing a new methodical nature to the investigations instead of McNulty’s brash confidence. But while the cops vs. criminals beats are familiar, they become just one part of a far more ambitious season that fully capitalized on Simon’s knowledge of the city. (Simon worked as a journalist at The Baltimore Sun for 12 years before creating The Wire.)

The season’s largest secondary plot involves the students at a Baltimore middle school where former cop Roland Pryzbylewski (Jim True-Frost) begins teaching. The young actors do a phenomenal job establishing likable characters in terrible circumstances. You want to root for all of them, from the ambitious Randy Wagstaff (Maestro Harrell) to the fiercely protective Michael Lee (Tristan Wilds). By the end of season 4, the system has profoundly failed almost all of these kids, driving them to lives of addiction and violence. The Wire was always good at making most of its characters sympathetic, but seeing children trapped in such a brutal cycle really drives home the show’s themes of how crime thrives when institutions fail.

That theme also plays out in the mayoral campaign of Tommy Carcetti (Aidan Gillen). Carcetti was introduced in season 3 as an ambitious city council member, but he really shines in season 4 as he runs on reforming the Baltimore Police Department — and then quickly breaks his promises. Gillen’s oily performance as a political survivor who would doom his city to satisfy his own ambitions was what landed him the role of Littlefinger in Game of Thrones. Carcetti is even more compelling a character because he feels like a man who could have done good if he’d been willing to make sacrifices rather than feed his own ego.

The Wire season 4 is able to build so many new arcs because of the strong foundation established in the previous three seasons. The show is dense with rich characters who have grown significantly over the years even as they continue to struggle with many of the same problems. The cops and drug dealers alike feel world-weary as they deal with a more violent new generation represented by Marlo Stanfield (Jamie Hector) and his vicious enforcers Snoop (Felicia Pearson) and Chris Partlow (Gbenga Akinnagbe). Combined with the dark fate of most of the kids and Carcetti’s broken promises, the season creates the bleak feeling that everyone is trapped in a cycle of misery and decay.

Season 5 gave most of The Wire’s characters satisfying ends to their arcs and delivered a scathing critique of the downfall of American newspapers, commentary that has only become more profound 20 years later. But overall, it’s a big step down from season 4, spending too much time on a goofy fake serial killer plot and not enough on the nuanced character-driven stories that made season 4 feel so authentic. There are no bad seasons of The Wire, but season 4 perfectly achieved the show’s goals. It’s an alternately funny, tragic, and profound look at the complex ways that people are betrayed by systems that never really give them a chance to succeed.
